boys device probe | device 0 | Quadro T1000 | count 262144 | seed 47 | status 0
boys device option probe - this result is about this device, this build, this process and the workload below.
  device: Quadro T1000
    ordinal 0, compute capability 7.5, 14 streaming multiprocessors
    memory 4.0 GiB, graphics clock 1.46 GHz, memory clock 4.00 GHz
    single:double throughput ratio 32:1
    driver 13.2, runtime 13.3, built with toolkit 13.3.73, for architecture(s) 75

  workload:
    262144 arguments per call, and 1048576 at the pair count (the wider of the two every row
    is read at), log-uniform over [0.001, 40], each carrying its own highest
    order drawn as the sum of two shell angular momenta 0..7, capped at 32,
    sorted by argument so the uniform-order entries' stated precondition holds.
    the fp32 and fp16 device-callable entries take the argument rounded to their own
    precision, which is what a caller's kernel holds in a register; the batch entries
    of those lanes take the double argument and round it themselves.

  protocol:
    5 passes, 2 rounds per pass, 16 launches per timed region. Every entry is timed by
    a CUDA event pair around those launches, into buffers uploaded once before the first
    clock; the per-argument reading is the device time divided by the launches and the
    arguments.
    each entry is read twice per round, at 262144 and at 1048576 arguments, and its figure is the
    count-independent cost the two readings extrapolate to: a launch term L paid once per
    launch enters a reading at C as L/C, so with the second count r times the first the two
    readings fix the term and the cost the entry is left with is (r*f' - f)/(r - 1). Both readings are
    reported beside the figure. **Nothing an empty kernel costs is subtracted**: the
    extrapolation is the entry's own, and the floor the control states is the cheaper
    launch a kernel that does no arithmetic makes.
    every row is timed once per round and the visit order is shuffled per round, so the 10
    rounds of the run are pooled into one table of 10 rows and 1056 columns — one row per
    round and one column per row of the measurement table. A comparison between two entries is
    the ratio of their cells in one row: both were timed under whatever clock that round ran
    at, and a drift common to the round cancels in the ratio. Every row of the run is timed under
    the one set of degree tables this run made resident before any row was timed, so any
    two entries of a comparison were timed under one arithmetic.
    no round and no pass is dropped. A reported figure is the middle of those ratios
    and never the minimum of the run: on a card whose clock decays the minimum is
    the earliest and best-clocked round, which is not what a caller's long workload
    meets, and a central ratio credits the reference entry and its rivals alike; the
    upper quartile of that ratio is printed beside the figure so both ends come from
    one distribution. The peak column is the entry's own fastest single round, a raw
    figure under no anchor and not a bound on the columns beside it.
    the canary beside a pass is a diagnostic and gates nothing: a fixed work read by a
    device clock measures the clock as much as the load, so a pass is flagged when the
    canary's own runs disagreed by more than 5.0% and is used either way.
    in-kernel rows are a subtraction: the caller's kernel with the entry in it, less the
    same kernel with the call removed and the traffic kept, the two timed adjacent in
    the same round, so the difference is formed inside that round and survives a drift.

  canary:
    a fixed-work integer kernel, launched between the rounds, holding no floating-point
    state, so the arithmetic this probe ranks cannot change the instrument's own cost.
    its fastest run seen here is 2.7891 ms; the widest spread across the passes is 35.30%,
    beside the widest within-round paired spread of 200.00%. Both are context: the
    ordering below is made in the paired column, and the canary is the same fixed work
    read by the same clock the entries were.

pass  wall_s  canary_ms  canary%  paired%  verdict

   4  379.235     3.4163    12.22   200.00  canary wide - reported, used

  5 of 5 pass(es) ran with the canary's own runs wider than the 5.0% alarm, and every
  one of them was used: the canary is fixed work read by the same clock the entries were,
  so it cannot separate the two, and discarding on it would discard the measurement
  rather than the machine. What separates the entries is the paired column and the
  refusal below, not this one.

  The paired column is the widest within-round band any entry of that pass showed, so
  it is the resolution that pass alone can order at.
  A pass holds two rounds and the two ends of a quartile band over two
  readings are three times apart at the extreme, so 200.00% is that column's
  own ceiling: a pass reading it had one entry whose two rounds were as far
  apart as two readings can be.
  The run's resolution, below, is the same measurement over all 10 pooled rounds.

entries - ns/argument is the arithmetic's own device time per argument, transfer and
  host submission excluded, and each row's figure is formed from that row's own two
  readings: the column headed 262144 is the row at 262144 arguments per call, the column
  headed 1048576 is the same row at 1048576 arguments, and 'ns/arg' is the count-independent
  cost the two extrapolate to. A launch costs what it costs once per launch, so the
  share of a reading that is the launcher falls as 1/count: with the pair count r times
  the first, readings f and f' leave the entry (r*f' - f)/(r - 1). That the launch term
  falls as 1/count is the one assumption the extrapolation makes, and both readings are
  printed so that what came out of a figure can be checked rather than taken on trust.
  The device-side launch
  floor - a kernel launched the same way that does no Boys arithmetic - is 28.442 us per
  launch here, 0.1085 ns/argument at the first of those counts. It is the cheapest launch
  this route can make, and it is not what came out of the figures: an entry's own
  kernel starts more work than an empty one, so its launch term is the larger one the
  two readings fixed for it.

  device-all-orders-bfloat16-orders-mono-fast-plain-reciprocal bf16       all-orders  in-kernel      11.106      10.771      10.637    10.881  1.836      1.06x  4.040..4.281   -3.06%    10.277  10/10      1e-07  caller kernel, subtracted
  device-all-orders-bfloat16-orders-mono-fast bf16       all-orders  in-kernel      11.095      10.681      10.722    11.071  1.852      1.06x  4.100..4.356   -2.26%    10.552  10/10      1e-07  caller kernel, subtracted

  the three cost columns and the two readings are the reference entry's own lower-quartile
  cost over this entry's 10 paired rounds, scaled by this entry's ratio to it: the two
  readings at the lower quartile of that ratio, the figure in ns/arg at its middle
  ('single-fp64', whose own ratio is exactly 1.000 and whose drift is exactly 0.00%) — so the two
  readings and the figure are anchored alike from the same rounds, and the figure is the
  reading column it sits beside with the launch term the two readings fixed taken out of it. A
  launched row's readings both contain that term, the second with a
  quarter of it, which is why the second is the cheaper of the two; an
  in-kernel row's readings are differences between two halves that both carried the
  launch, so its two columns sit on top of each other and its figure barely moves. 'hi' is
  the figure at the upper quartile of the same rounds and 'spread' is hi over lo, so the
  two ends of a row come from one distribution. The vs-ref band is that ratio over the
  middle half of the rounds, and drift is how far it moved between the run's first and
  second half — a row whose drift is larger than the resolution below is a row whose
  two entries do not carry this card's clock alike. 'peak ns' is the fastest single round
  the entry was ever seen in: one round's own figure under no anchor, the entry's own
  floor and not a bound on the columns beside it, which are anchored to the
  reference entry — and a round whose cell was zero is left out of it, because such a
  cell is not a cost. 'rounds' is how many of the run's pooled rounds the row rests on, and
  every measured row rests on every one of them — no round and no pass is dropped for it.

  the minus column is the second half of a subtraction: the same caller-shaped kernel with
  the call removed and the traffic kept, timed in the same round as the half it is
  subtracted from, so the difference is formed inside that round and a drift common to
  the round cancels in it. Both columns are then aggregated over the same rounds. A
  launched row has no minus column because nothing was subtracted from it. The ns/arg
  column reads unresolved for an in-kernel row whose difference is not positive at
the lower quartile of its own rounds - the quantile the two readings beside it are
  stated at - because what such a row would print there is the instrument's floor
  and not a cost.

  the bound column is what each entry's lane documents, read from the library's own report
  of the option space and not measured here. What a lane delivers on this card is the accuracy
  gate's business. The bounds in that column are not all the same, and a row that carries a
  looser one is a row that bought speed with accuracy: the classes below are per precision
  and per question shape, so no row is ordered against a row of another class, and the name a class
  carries is reached among its own rows and read at the bound each of them states.

  64 launches: 0.521 ns/argument

  floor, a kernel launched the same way with no Boys arithmetic in it: 28.442 us per launch

  an entry's own kernel costs more to start than this empty one, and each row's launch
  term is the one its own two readings fixed. A row whose whole figure is below this
  line is launch-bound.

rankings - one class per precision and question shape.
  A class is what the caller has already fixed when they make the call: how much
  precision the result needs, and what they are asking for. This run's option table
  carries 1056 row(s) across 12 class(es), and no entry of one class was ordered against
  an entry of another. What varies inside a class is what the library picks on the
  caller's behalf, and the division form, which the caller may name and the build's
  own default supplies when they name none.

class fp32, all-orders
  every entry in this class runs fp32, and answers the question "a ladder per argument: F_0(x)..F_n(x) at that argument's own order n". The class is one precision and one question shape, because those two are what the caller has already chosen when they make the call: no entry of this class is an alternative to one of another class, and no entry of it was ordered against one. What varies inside the class is what the library picks on the caller's behalf — the fit route, the evaluation scheme, the partition and the packing axis — and the division form, which the caller may name and which the build's own default supplies when they name none. So its winner is the fastest fp32 row of this question, at the bound its own row states, and the bound column above is that figure. This class holds 240 row(s) of the option table here. They do not document one bound between them: a row of a looser bound is a faster way to answer this question at that accuracy, and both stand in the one ranking, which is what makes it a ranking by cost.

class fp16, all-orders
  every entry in this class runs fp16, and answers the question "a ladder per argument: F_0(x)..F_n(x) at that argument's own order n". The class is one precision and one question shape, because those two are what the caller has already chosen when they make the call: no entry of this class is an alternative to one of another class, and no entry of it was ordered against one. What varies inside the class is what the library picks on the caller's behalf — the fit route, the evaluation scheme, the partition and the packing axis — and the division form, which the caller may name and which the build's own default supplies when they name none. So its winner is the fastest fp16 row of this question, at the bound its own row states, and the bound column above is that figure. This class holds 240 row(s) of the option table here. They document one bound between them, so the ranking is at one accuracy.

class bf16, all-n
  every entry in this class runs bf16, and answers the question "one common ladder for the whole batch: F_0(x)..F_nmax(x) for every argument". The class is one precision and one question shape, because those two are what the caller has already chosen when they make the call: no entry of this class is an alternative to one of another class, and no entry of it was ordered against one. What varies inside the class is what the library picks on the caller's behalf — the fit route, the evaluation scheme, the partition and the packing axis — and the division form, which the caller may name and which the build's own default supplies when they name none. So its winner is the fastest bf16 row of this question, at the bound its own row states, and the bound column above is that figure. This class holds 12 row(s) of the option table here. They document one bound between them, so the ranking is at one accuracy.

This ranking is about this card: Quadro T1000, compute capability 7.5. It documents a ratio of single- to double-precision throughput of 32:1, so how far the fp64 lane sits behind the fp32 lane here is this card's ratio and not the library's -- a part with a ratio of 2 orders those two differently, and a figure taken here would be the wrong one there. Its compute capability predates the bf16 tensor instructions, so nothing here runs on a tensor path and none of these figures is a tensor figure. A different card, a different toolkit or a different set of target architectures can rank these entries differently, which is why the probe is run where the numbers are used rather than read from documentation.

  rows this run carries that the library's report does not: none
  a refused row is refused by the library where it is named, and not by this probe: the
  option is absent from the table above because no build with this seam serves it, and the
  report states the seam. An option added to the library reaches this table without an edit
  here; one removed leaves it.

not measured by this probe, by design: the ordered-batch sort (the AllN entries are
  handed a batch that already satisfies their stated precondition, so nothing here is a
  figure for sorting one); the arithmetic's accuracy (the device accuracy gate measures
  that, against the committed reference). The accuracy figures themselves are not absent
  from this report: each row's bound column states the figure its own lane documents, read
  from the library's table rather than measured here.
